# Goose as an agent-written Sofort computer: measured spike

Date: 2026-10-25. Lane: hands-on Goose. **M** = measured/exercised in this spike; **S** = sourced from inspected upstream/local research; **I** = inference; **E** = engineering estimate. Every numeric table below is **M** unless explicitly marked otherwise. Times are local microbenchmarks, not service latency or Cloudflare edge measurements. Bytes are decimal bytes; KiB = 1,024 bytes.

## Decision

**I — Worth a narrow compiler/runtime experiment; do not replace Sofort's JS path or promise upstream Goose-on-Workers today.** A generated, specialized Goose computer can be dramatically smaller than carrying QuickJS. But upstream does not yet support Wasm, its C backend assumes 64-bit layouts, and flat data does not serialize an active C/Wasm control stack. The measured port below is a deliberately limited research adapter, with unsafe fixed partitions, not a qualified sandbox runtime.

**M — What worked:** five programs compiled to C, native Apple arm64, static Linux aarch64/musl, and adapted wasm32-wasip1. All five produced expected outputs in native macOS, native Linux, Node and Wasmtime. Local workerd ran the text task and restored the parked program. A Node-origin checkpoint forked in fresh Node, Wasmtime and workerd instances with two different replies, producing the independently checked results and no duplicate tool requests.

**M — Central numbers:** the adapted modules are **1,317–47,973 bytes** raw, versus the prior QuickJS reactor's **966,584 bytes [S]**. The parked Goose image is **1,572,864 bytes raw / 13,462 bytes gzip-9**, or **32,816 bytes / 10,780 bytes gzip-9** with a tested nonzero-page encoding, plus **239 bytes** of JSON metadata. In a matched array/checksum program, QuickJS snapshots are **110,562–111,332 bytes gzip-9**. However, XOR deltas against initialized memory are **13,230 bytes for Goose versus 21,480–22,762 for QuickJS**: much of the full-image advantage is the missing interpreter and initialization state, not uniquely flat values.

**I — The differentiated story is “tiny compiled, forkable state with a complete effects boundary,” not “Goose makes pause trivial.”** C/Rust-to-Wasm plus Asyncify can do the same control-stack trick; Golem already offers durable Wasm; Sofort's own QuickJS prototype already moves continuations. Goose's useful contribution is its data representation and potential compiler-supported checkpoints. A credible upstream contribution with hostile-input growth checks would be more interesting to Wouter than an unsupported language-replacement pitch.

**M:** Apple M4 Pro, 24 GiB RAM, arm64 Darwin 25.6.0. Work was concurrent with other research, so compare broad effects, not tiny differences. All spike files are under `/Users/noah/code/sofort-spikes/goose/`. Only this report was written in the Sofort repository. The pre-existing kernel work and portability report were not modified. No accounts, login, messages, deployments or Cloudflare resources were created. workerd listened on localhost only and was stopped after testing.

## Upstream: impressive young compiler, not a mature portable runtime

**S/M:** Inspected the specification (especially memory model, copying, lifetimes, compilation and representation), tutorial, compiler implementation notes, runtime and code generator, Wasm design proposal, README, license, CI/release workflows, git history, releases and all issues/PRs returned by GitHub's first 100-item page. The snapshot had 82 issue/PR numbers, so this bounded request covered the current open list. Raw API replies are `repo.json`, `issues.json`, `releases.json`, `ci.json` in the spike.

* **S:** The pipeline is parse → resolve → monomorphizing typecheck → optimize → bounds-check elimination → C emission → either external C compiler or bundled TinyCC. Most passes are header-inline C++ in one translation unit. Fixed locals can live in registers/native frames; dynamic values use compiler-assigned data stacks. This is a native compiler, not a bytecode interpreter. [Pinned implementation](https://github.com/aardappel/goose/blob/e76d5c9f7f0a17d83c12a405fbb2513094d03985/docs/implementation.md).
* **S:** “Flat” is a property of selected types, not every Goose value. The spec excludes references, slices **and relative references** from flat values. Programs commonly use these other types; only flat values cross typed thread queues. Native pointer-containing runtime state still exists. [Pinned spec §1](https://github.com/aardappel/goose/blob/e76d5c9f7f0a17d83c12a405fbb2513094d03985/docs/goose_spec.md#1-core-concepts).
* **M/S:** Git has **905 commits**, starting with the spec on **2026-08-27**; **869** attributed to Wouter (96.0%). There are **14** distinct author names in `git shortlog` (not necessarily distinct humans). Current HEAD's author date is **2026-10-23**; the GitHub API's latest repository push is **2026-10-25**, which can include other branches. The brief supplies the October 8 announcement date; I did not independently establish that date. The API reported **1,946 stars, 71 forks, 12 open items**; those 12 include **5 issues and 7 PRs**, not 12 defects. [Repository API](https://api.github.com/repos/aardappel/goose), [pinned history](https://github.com/aardappel/goose/commits/e76d5c9f7f0a17d83c12a405fbb2513094d03985/).
* **M/S:** Three non-prerelease tags returned: **v2026.1 October 14, v2026.2 October 16, v2026.3 October 21**. `wc -l src/*.h src/*.cpp src/runtime/*.h` is **65,873 physical lines**, including comments; the brief's “~30k” is not the current count under that definition. The README credits Claude Fable with compiler implementation, benchmarks, samples and docs; that is the author's statement, not an independent provenance audit. [Releases](https://github.com/aardappel/goose/releases), [README history/AI section](https://github.com/aardappel/goose/blob/e76d5c9f7f0a17d83c12a405fbb2513094d03985/README.md#history--use-of-ai).
* **S:** CI specifies Windows/macOS/Linux compiler and generated-C tests, O0/O2, error/roundtrip cases, TinyCC, sample tests, extension/LSP tests, and a separate Linux ASan/UBSan profile. The inspected HEAD's CI run succeeded. The API's latest ten workflow records contain both successful and failed runs and approval-required external PR runs. I built locally and exercised the five samples; **I did not run the full upstream suite**. [Pinned CI](https://github.com/aardappel/goose/blob/e76d5c9f7f0a17d83c12a405fbb2513094d03985/.github/workflows/ci.yml), [CI runs](https://github.com/aardappel/goose/actions/workflows/ci.yml).
* **S:** Open issue **#82** gives a reproducible valid-looking program whose generated C fails to compile; **#63** asks about generating libraries; **#69** concerns namespace lookup. Recent closed defects include typechecking not settling, a global resizable-stack interaction and thread/global string behavior. These show active development and real compiler edges, not proof that the whole language is unsafe. [#82](https://github.com/aardappel/goose/issues/82), [#63](https://github.com/aardappel/goose/issues/63), [#69](https://github.com/aardappel/goose/issues/69), [#32](https://github.com/aardappel/goose/issues/32), [#34](https://github.com/aardappel/goose/issues/34), [#31](https://github.com/aardappel/goose/issues/31).
* **S:** Main license is Apache-2.0. The mandatory embedded TinyCC has its own LGPL-2.1 licensing/relicensing notes. Generated native/Wasm applications in this spike do not link TinyCC; the compiler does. Do not describe the entire bundled compiler as Apache-only. [Goose LICENSE](https://github.com/aardappel/goose/blob/e76d5c9f7f0a17d83c12a405fbb2513094d03985/LICENSE), [TinyCC notice](https://github.com/aardappel/goose/blob/e76d5c9f7f0a17d83c12a405fbb2513094d03985/third_party/tinycc/README.md).

**I:** Good research dependency with unusually thorough design material and test intent; poor foundation for a promise of stable ABI, universal compiler safety or a production agent language today. Concentrated maintenance and very young releases are material. “AI wrote it” neither establishes nor refutes correctness.

## Building and the real wasm32 blockers

**M:** Cloned upstream into the new, empty `upstream/` subdirectory. Read `CMakeLists.txt`, `cmake/libtcc.cmake` and the package configuration path before configuring. Built without `GOOSE_BUILTIN_LIBS=all`, avoiding configure-time fetches of graphics/physics packages. The compiler built successfully, with four warnings in the final C++ compile. Build logs are `configure.log` and `build.log`.

**M:** The compiler located outside the source tree initially could not find stdlib. Passing `--stdlib upstream/stdlib` fixed this. Stock emitted C plus stock emitted runtime compiled and ran on Apple arm64. Zig built static musl binaries for all five; all ran correctly in the existing Linux VM.

**M:** Compiling the stock emitted runtime for WASI failed. The first errors were unsupported `setjmp.h`, no true `mmap`, no signals, missing `siginfo_t` / `sigaction`; later native threading/OS assumptions remain. Enabling emulation headers cannot supply safe unmapped guard gaps inside a Wasm linear memory. `logs/stock-wasi.log` records the actual diagnostics.

**S:** The upstream Wasm note explicitly presents a proposal rather than an implemented backend. Its first required step is bounds-checked stack growth. [Pinned Wasm proposal](https://github.com/aardappel/goose/blob/e76d5c9f7f0a17d83c12a405fbb2513094d03985/docs/design/wasm.md).

**M/S:** A second independent blocker is the backend's explicit **64-bit, little-endian** representation contract (`docs/implementation.md`, representation section). With a naive wasm32 adapter, JSON tree parsing reached the sample's integer-field assertion and trapped: the C pointer fields shrank while generated offsets retained 64-bit assumptions. [Pinned representation](https://github.com/aardappel/goose/blob/e76d5c9f7f0a17d83c12a405fbb2513094d03985/docs/implementation.md#6-code-generation-representation).

**M — Adapter actually measured:**

1. Preserve generated program logic and inline arithmetic/check helpers. Replace platform runtime operations with `adapter.c`: **16 static partitions of 65,536 bytes**, a small calloc arena for runtime descriptors, output capture, panic-as-trap, scalar byte search, and the original formatting routines copied to `format.inc`.
2. `pad_layout.py` wraps plain pointer fields in anonymous unions with 64-bit storage under `__wasm__`, preserving the selected structures' native-sized fields and positional C initializers. An earlier padding-field experiment broke compound initializers; final builds use unions and fail immediately on a compiler error. This is a **sample-specific representation shim**, not a general 32-bit backend: pointer arrays, arbitrary FFI and all layouts are not qualified.
3. Compile using WASI SDK with **128 KiB shadow stack, 1.5 MiB initial linear memory, 16 MiB maximum**. These reactors have **zero WASI imports** after dead-code elimination: compute/text/aggregation/JSON import nothing; the snapshot sample imports only `sofort.tool_call(i64)->i64`. “Built with a WASI toolchain” does not mean Cloudflare supplied full WASI.
4. Native warm-compute comparisons use the **same adapter and C logic**, compiled into a dylib, to avoid measuring native CLI launch/stdio against a reused Wasm instance. Native Linux syscall tests use the **unmodified stock runtime**, not this adapter.

**I — Critical qualification:** The adapter lacks per-growth bounds checks. A Goose data stack can overrun its own partition and corrupt another region before reaching the Wasm memory boundary. It is unsuitable for hostile/agent input. It does not falsify Goose's native safety model, which relies on guard pages; it demonstrates exactly why a supported Wasm port needs compiler work. There is no arbitrary code/package/extern sandbox policy implemented here.

## Five programs, sizes and compilation latency

**M:** The three realistic tasks were written during this spike after reading docs/examples, rather than copied from existing sample programs. Inputs are deliberately small and fixed, so these are integration/microbenchmark cases, not representative application throughput. The two additional programs exercise compute and parking.

| Program | Work and checked output |
| --- | --- |
| `json_transform.goose` | Parse two JSON order rows using stdlib JSON tree; validate integer fields; emit `[{"id":11,"total":3998},{"id":12,"total":1500}]` |
| `aggregate.goose` | Parse CSV-like records; group into a slice-key dictionary; emit `eu:2:4000`, `us:2:750`, `ap:1:300` in deterministic key order |
| `text.goose` | Trim log lines, select errors, redact tokens; emit two correctly redacted error messages |
| `compute.goose` | Two million LCG iterations and checksum; answer `65537700344` |
| `snapshot.goose` | Build 2,048 i64 records from a 32-bit LCG; retain array across tool import; use reply-selected element after resumption |

All sizes are the final artifacts. Wasm uses the adapter; native stock-runtime outputs are separate builds. Static binaries are stripped; Mach-O executables are not stripped. gzip uses level 9 and deterministic timestamp 0.

| Program | C bytes | Mach-O bytes | Static musl bytes / gzip | Wasm bytes / gzip |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| json_transform | 284677 | 126552 | 130,592 / 77,308 | 47,973 / 24,030 |
| aggregate | 123599 | 93136 | 102,568 / 56,654 | 8,403 / 3,770 |
| text | 77732 | 92856 | 99,688 / 55,264 | 4,159 / 2,195 |
| compute | 61324 | 92768 | 97,920 / 54,015 | 1,317 / 954 |
| snapshot | 63200 | 92768 | 98,008 / 54,110 | 1,811 / 1,118 |


**M:** The Asyncify snapshot module is **2,221 raw / 1,277 gzip-9 bytes**, versus **1,811 / 1,118** untransformed: **+22.6% raw / +14.2% gzip**. It includes three explicitly exported mutable globals for checkpointing. This tiny program does not establish Asyncify overhead for a large Goose module.

**M:** Source-to-C and adapted-C-to-Wasm compile wall times, three compiler process samples each (no package downloads, no Asyncify). Adapter generation itself is excluded. Values below are medians; all raw observations and exact compiler argument arrays are in the appendix.

| Program | Goose → C median ms | Adapted C → Wasm median ms |
| --- | --- | --- |
| json_transform | 36.211 | 1501.372 |
| aggregate | 13.726 | 349.577 |
| text | 19.156 | 308.685 |
| compute | 6.039 | 239.728 |
| snapshot | 8.361 | 241.549 |


**I:** This compile stage is a real tradeoff: QuickJS accepts new JS without compiling a new Wasm module. TinyCC's native in-process mode does not compile Wasm. Cache compiled code by exact source/compiler/stdlib/ABI/options identity; an agent retrying new programs still pays compilation latency and uses the trusted compiler service.

## Runtime measurements and QuickJS comparison

**M — Protocol:** Three sequential process rounds per runtime. Per program/round: **120 instantiate and `_initialize` observations**, 20 warm executions, then **7 batches** of 500 executions for small tasks or 10 executions for the two-million-iteration compute task. Tables use the median of the three within-round medians. Native dylib calls are through Python ctypes and include that call overhead. Wasmtime is the C API, not Python on the timed path. Node CPU time includes all process CPU, including background compilation; wall time is the clearer tiny-task metric.

`run()` resets the adapter and reinitializes the program's globals each time. `_initialize` is only the WASI reactor constructor and is almost empty; it is **not Goose program initialization**. Program initialization is inside `run`. Wasmtime's constructor frequently read below timer resolution; report it as sub-microsecond, not literally free. Precompiled instantiation includes store/export setup in the Wasmtime harness and export access in Node, but no source compilation, IPC, MCP, policy, network, storage or host effects.

| Program | Node instantiate ms | Wasmtime instantiate ms | Native run ms | Node run ms | Wasmtime run ms |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| json_transform | 0.027292 | 0.009000 | 0.000646 | 0.000920 | 0.000822 |
| aggregate | 0.023000 | 0.009000 | 0.000264 | 0.000345 | 0.000252 |
| text | 0.029500 | 0.009000 | 0.000206 | 0.000370 | 0.000290 |
| compute | 0.028042 | 0.009000 | 3.178417 | 0.971950 | 1.135700 |
| snapshot | 0.026875 | 0.009000 | 0.003384 | 0.001115 | 0.001300 |


**M:** Compute CPU medians: native **3.175 ms**, Node **0.971 ms**, Wasmtime **1.125 ms**; Wasm/native ratios **0.306× / 0.354×**. The Wasm backends were faster on this particular masked recurrence. Both returned the same exact checksum. Additional Apple clang 21 versus Homebrew clang 23 runs remained about 3.1–3.2 CPU-ms natively (`native-compiler-comparison.json`). Native assembly retains a scalar 64-bit multiply/add recurrence. This is a code-generation-sensitive integer toy, **not a general Goose-on-Wasm speedup** or a matched rerun of the prior QuickJS CPU workload.

**M:** Fresh-process Node test, **11 processes per task**, excludes Node process launch and reading the module from the displayed core total; the separate process total includes those. V8 can tier after module construction.

| Task | Compile ms | Instantiate ms | Init + first run ms | Core total ms | Process + I/O total ms |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| compute | 0.166 | 0.058 | 2.315 | 2.538 | 39.177 |
| json_transform | 0.240 | 0.058 | 0.458 | 0.765 | 37.171 |
| snapshot | 0.165 | 0.064 | 0.141 | 0.364 | 36.660 |


**S:** The prior [QuickJS report](../portability/02-wasm.md) used the same Mac but **Node 24.22.1 / V8 13.6**, not this spike's available Node 23.10 / V8 12.9. Its module is **966,584 raw / 332,133 gzip-9 bytes**; instantiate ≈**0.025 ms Wasmtime / 0.092 ms Node**, then QuickJS init ≈**0.13–0.16 / 0.207 ms**. Fresh Node compile+instantiate+init+first trivial run had **36.37 ms** median, excluding process launch and I/O. The prior tool RTT was ≈**45–77 µs**, including JSON/QuickJS promise handling. Its compute was **1.4× slower than native in V8 and 2.2–2.3× in Wasmtime** on a different workload. These sourced figures are context, not a controlled claim of a precise Goose speedup.

**I:** The decisive qualitative advantage is eliminating the interpreter module/context when the program is known ahead of time. It is not correct to compare Goose's bare integer import or sub-microsecond fixed-input task against QuickJS's JSON tool-dispatch RTT and call that an executor improvement.

## Linear memory, resident memory and peaks

**M:** For all five adapted modules, initial **and observed peak linear memory are 1,572,864 bytes**; no `memory.grow` occurs in these tasks. The **16,777,216-byte maximum** is a configured outer cap, not measured usage. Each partition is only 64 KiB even when there is unused space elsewhere. The 16 partitions are a convenience for the largest sample, not a compiler-derived minimum. The source snapshot array payload alone is **16,384 bytes**.

**M:** Memory runs retained **32 or 128 executed instances**, three independent processes each, sharing a compiled module. Node used explicit GC; Wasmtime deleted one warmed instance before measuring and used default allocation/CoW. The table uses the **128-instance** medians, because Node's 32-instance deltas were negative for JSON/snapshot due to GC/residency changes. Small RSS deltas are not a hard quota or proof of adversarial density.

| Task | Node resident bytes / instance | Wasmtime resident bytes / instance | Node process before → after bytes | Wasmtime process before → after bytes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| compute | 46208 | 56320 | 42,844,160 → 48,807,936 | 11,091,968 → 18,268,160 |
| snapshot | 39808 | 72960 | 46,137,344 → 51,314,688 | 11,452,416 → 20,774,912 |
| json_transform | 113920 | 105216 | 46,743,552 → 61,390,848 | 24,707,072 → 38,174,720 |


**M:** Node's maximum process high-water RSS observed in those 128-instance runs was **61,784,064 bytes**. Wasmtime measurements are current resident size before/after retention, **not a sampled lifetime RSS peak**; all raw readings are preserved. Linear-memory peak is known independently because the module never grows it. Linux `/usr/bin/time -v` reported **400 KiB maximum RSS for every tiny static program**; that identical coarse VM reading is retained as an observation, not evidence of exact per-instance resident cost. Native virtual reservations are much larger than resident use.

**S/I:** QuickJS's prior marginal resident medians were **332,800–400,640 bytes per initialized instance**, with **2 MiB logical memory** and **69–99 MiB shared runtime-process baselines**. Goose's measured **~39–111 KiB Node / ~55–103 KiB Wasmtime** per small instance is lower here, but versions, inputs and host conditions differ. It is plausible to pack compiled small capsules more densely; production saturation/resource limits and hostile programs were not tested. No cloud cost/throughput extrapolation is justified by this alone.

## Snapshot, cross-runtime restore and two forks

**M — Actual parked computation:** `snapshot.goose` creates 2,048 i64 values with `x=(x*1664525+1013904223)%4294967296`, computes sum **4,462,528,222,208**, and enters `tool_call(sum)`. It keeps the array alive and subsequently returns `sum + rows[reply % rows.len] + rows[2047] + reply`. Thus the reply chooses a data-dependent retained element; this is not simply a precomputed answer plus reply. An independent Python implementation checked both results.

**M — Untransformed failure:** A host callback copied linear memory and exported globals, then threw to simulate suspension. A fresh instance restored those bytes, but its answer was still zero and it had no continuation entry. Calling `run()` restarted the program and issued **one duplicate tool call**. Memory plus globals do not retain Wasm operand/control frames. This negative test is `snapshot-extra.mjs` / `logs/snapshot-extra.json`.

**M — Successful mechanism:** Binaryen Asyncify transforms calls that can reach the host import. The host import starts unwind and returns a placeholder; generated code returns to the host. After `asyncify_stop_unwind`, the Wasm stack is quiescent. Dump **all linear memory and all three mutable globals**. The Wasm disassembly was inspected, and each mutable global was explicitly exported; `__stack_pointer` aliases the first. No table is present. The serialized Asyncify control payload is **32 bytes**, within a separately reserved **64 KiB** save area. The globals are `{stack_pointer:131072, asyncify_state:0, asyncify_data:1196272}`. The JSON envelope also records the data pointer and request checksum.

To restore: instantiate the **same exact transformed module**, run reactor constructor, copy memory, restore globals, set a new branch's reply, call `asyncify_start_rewind(data)`, then `run()`. The host import recognizes rewind, stops it, supplies the reply, and does not reissue a request. Only a copy/rewind is tested; no durable effect ledger, leases, authentication or real approval service is present. [Asyncify implementation and control-stack model](https://github.com/WebAssembly/binaryen/blob/version_133/src/passes/Asyncify.cpp).

| Fresh destination | Reply 19 result | Reply 77 result | New tool requests |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Node/V8 | 4466325887613 | 4465308209465 | 0 |
| Wasmtime 50.0.1 | 4466325887613 | 4465308209465 | 0 |
| Local workerd | 4466325887613 | 4465308209465 | 0 |


**M:** Node-origin checkpoint bytes and measured timings:

| Observation | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Raw linear image | 1,572,864 bytes |
| Full image gzip-9 | 13,462 bytes |
| Nonzero bytes | 8383 |
| Nonzero pages | 8 × 4 KiB; 3 × 64 KiB |
| Sparse image raw / gzip-9 | 32,816 / 10,780 bytes |
| JSON metadata raw / gzip-9 | 239 / 161 bytes |
| First suspend incl. work | 0.420709 ms (single sample) |
| Node memory copy | 0.005333 ms (single sample) |
| Node restore copies ms | [0.1717909999999847, 0.12649999999999295] |
| Wasmtime restore copies ms | [0.522084, 0.218625] |


**M:** The sparse encoding includes a 16-byte header plus page index and 4,096-byte payload for each nonzero page. Its page indexes are **32, 34, 35, 36, 37, 38, 292, 308**. Reconstructing a zero-filled image from this encoding was checked byte-for-byte against the full original; the already tested restore path then consumes that exact image. Metadata is additional, and the immutable module must be retained/content-addressed separately. A raw RAM dump need not equal the amount of live logical data; dead/stale bytes can remain in stack memory.

**M:** Warm Asyncify suspend+rewind with immediate reply (no memory copy, gzip, transport or storage) had median **0.001173 ms**, seven batches of 1,000 after 1,000 warmups. It includes the same array construction as `run()`. Snapshot/restore copy figures in the appendix are only one/two observations, not percentiles. Full-image gzip-9 itself had **23.431 ms median, n=15**, and dominates these tiny execution times. Sparse/delta compression and off-thread storage are important engineering choices; they were not latency-benchmarked here.

### Is flat state actually smaller than QuickJS?

**S:** The earlier portability snapshot was **2,097,152 raw / 99,854 gzip bytes** and retained a small JS closure, cycle, Map and typed array. It is a different program.

**M:** Reused its exact QuickJS Wasm module read-only and wrote a new matched test in this spike: same LCG, 2,048 retained numeric elements, checksum, selected element after await, and both fork replies. Tested both an ordinary JS array and a Float64Array (same 8-byte numeric payload width as Goose i64, with exact integer values in this range). All forks returned the same expected answers. A separate empty-await baseline distinguishes interpreter cost from payload cost. gzip-9 and XOR-against-initialized-memory deltas are measured with the same Python compression method; Node's Goose full-image gzip result has the same byte count.

| Program | Raw bytes | Full gzip-9 bytes | XOR initialized-base gzip-9 bytes | Nonzero 4 KiB pages |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Goose + Asyncify | 1572864 | 13462 | 13230 | 8 |
| QuickJS empty | 2097152 | 98355 | 5547 | 68 |
| QuickJS array | 2097152 | 111332 | 22762 | 73 |
| QuickJS Float64Array | 2097152 | 110562 | 21480 | 72 |


**I:** Full images are roughly **8.2–8.3× smaller compressed** for the compiled Goose example. The initialized-base XOR deltas are only **1.62–1.72× smaller**. The incremental full-image compressed cost over empty QuickJS await is **12,207–12,977 bytes**, close to Goose's **11,769-byte** increase over its fresh reactor baseline (13,462 minus 1,693). Compression differences are not additive decompositions of heap use, but these observations strongly caution against attributing the entire full-image gap to flat layout. Interpreter tables, bytecode/compiler machinery, allocations and metadata dominate the baseline. Shared immutable initialized images/deltas narrow the gap; fixed numeric arrays in many compiled languages can make similarly small snapshots.

**I:** Goose does make contiguous state convenient and avoids a tracing GC. It does **not** remove native/Wasm call frames, runtime allocator/descriptors, pointer-width/ABI issues, effect ownership, or the need for a versioned module-specific checkpoint. A native aarch64 RAM image cannot be restored as wasm32 memory. Arbitrary mid-instruction suspension, recursive stacks, multiple pending tools, worker threads, mobile, x86 destinations and native jitk snapshots were not tested.

## Static Linux syscall surface versus jitk's 24 slots

**M:** All five **stock-runtime static musl binaries** executed successfully under strace in the existing Linux VM. Each used the same **13 unique syscall names including execve**; excluding the launcher's execve leaves **12** during guest execution. They did not create threads or touch files/network. Raw traces and stdout/stderr are retained under `linux/`.

| Syscall | Example compute call count | Current jitk relationship, from read-only inspection |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| `execve` | 1 | Linux trace launcher only; not required when jitk directly loads ELF |
| `set_tid_address` | 1 | Slot 96 exists |
| `rt_sigprocmask` | 1 | Slot 135 exists but intentionally returns ENOSYS |
| `rt_sigaction` | 2 | Slot 134 exists but intentionally returns ENOSYS; Goose needs real handlers |
| `sched_getaffinity` | 1 | No slot |
| `mmap` | 4 | Slot 222 exists; native Goose requests 2 GiB usable + 1 MiB gap per region |
| `munmap` | 3 | Slot 215 exists |
| `sigaltstack` | 2 | No slot |
| `mremap` | 33 | Slot 216 exists, documented ENOSYS fallback; Linux calls here also failed and musl continued |
| `mprotect` | 1 | No slot; native safety uses protected guard gaps |
| `madvise` | 1 | No slot; `MADV_NOHUGEPAGE` is used |
| `writev` | 1 | Slot 66 exists; musl output path |
| `exit_group` | 1 | Slot 94 maps to the single-thread exit implementation |

**M:** JSON used **7** protected stack regions, aggregation/text **2**, compute/snapshot **1**; each request was **2,148,532,224 bytes** including guard gap. These are virtual reservations, not resident RAM. Threads were not used; adding Goose's native worker queues would bring additional native thread/synchronization requirements.

**M:** Injecting `ENOSYS` into `rt_sigaction` with strace made the compute binary exit with `goose runtime error: cannot install data stack fault handler`. Thus even the two existing signal-number slots do not supply the semantics Goose needs. “12 syscalls < 24 slots” would be the wrong conclusion. The current [slot table](../../jitk/kernel/slots.json) and [kernel README](../../jitk/kernel/README.md) explicitly document those restricted handlers; no kernel source was changed.

**M/S:** Zig's default static ELF entry was **0x1015398**, with LOADs beginning at **0x1000000** and TLS present. The current loader documentation accepts LOAD VAs in **0x400000–0x680000** and has other page/permission constraints. These exact binaries were **not tested inside jitk** and need a conforming link layout as well as runtime changes. See `logs/elf-layout.txt` and the existing loader README.

**I:** For jitk, a small single-thread Goose runtime using bounded preallocated stacks and the tool channel is more plausible than emulating the entire desktop runtime. Safety needs explicit growth/shadow-stack bounds and resource enforcement. Simply shrinking `GS_STACK_RESERVE` does not provide missing signal/mprotect semantics. Keep native snapshot transport distinct from the portable Wasm capsule.

## Can an LLM write useful Goose?

**M:** This is one model's hands-on experience, **not a model benchmark**. After reading tutorial and stdlib examples, the three realistic source tasks compiled with one language-level correction: dictionary keys holding slices of string literals needed `dictionary<const u8[:], Totals>`, rather than `dictionary<u8[:], Totals>`. The compiler clearly explained the read-only slice mismatch and showed the generic instantiation chain. The initial stdlib-location failure was build setup, not language syntax.

**M:** JSON parsing/tree access and output formatting were straightforward once I inspected `json::parse`, `member` and `as_i64`. There is no ambient JS object/dictionary syntax: typed trees, optional narrowing and `(value, ok)` results require more boilerplate. Aggregation needed awareness that keys are borrowed slices with lifetimes and constness; deterministic output order was explicit. Text processing with `each_split`, `trim`, `starts_with` and slices was compact. Function-block captures were useful, but they are compile-time specialized blocks, not escaping async closures. None of these samples needed dynamic function pointers, recursion, real tool SDKs or a broad package ecosystem.

**I:** A supplied syntax/stdlib reference and a tight compile/run/fix loop make small agent-written tasks credible. This evidence does not establish success for an unprompted model, long programs or malformed hostile inputs. Goose being implemented with an LLM is separate evidence from LLMs being trained to write Goose. JS has a much larger existing tool/ecosystem/training advantage. Preserve JS as the default and offer Goose only for measured compute/data benefits.

The final task sources, with the original failed diagnostic preserved in `logs/aggregate-goose.log`:

### `json_transform.goose`

```goose
import std;
import json;
fn main() {
    let text = """[{"id":11,"qty":2,"cents":1999},{"id":12,"qty":3,"cents":500}]""";
    var doc: json::Node[>..] = [];
    var decoded: u8[>..] = [];
    let root, err = json::parse(text, doc, decoded);
    assert(root);
    var out: u8[>..] = [];
    out.push('[');
    var first = true;
    json::each(root) { row =>
        let id, idok = json::as_i64(json::member(row, "id"));
        let qty, qok = json::as_i64(json::member(row, "qty"));
        let cents, cok = json::as_i64(json::member(row, "cents"));
        assert(idok && qok && cok);
        if !first { out.push(','); }
        first = false;
        format(out, "{\"id\":", id, ",\"total\":", qty * cents, "}");
    };
    out.push(']');
    print(out);
}
```

### `aggregate.goose`

```goose
import std;
import dictionary;
struct Totals { count: i64, cents: i64 }
fn main() {
    let csv = "eu,1999\nus,500\neu,2001\nap,300\nus,250\n";
    var totals = dictionary<const u8[:], Totals> {};
    each_split(csv, '\n') { line =>
        if line.len > 0 {
            let comma = find(line, ",");
            assert(comma > 0);
            let cents, ok = parse_int(line[comma + 1..]);
            assert(ok);
            totals.update(line[..comma], Totals { 0, 0 }) { it.count++; it.cents += cents; };
        }
    };
    for key in ["eu", "us", "ap"] {
        let t = totals.get(key);
        assert(t);
        print(key, ":", t.count, ":", t.cents);
    }
}
```

### `text.goose`

```goose
import std;
fn main() {
    let log = " INFO ready\nERROR token=secret disk full\nwarning retry\nERROR token=other timeout\n";
    var out: u8[>..] = [];
    var count = 0;
    each_split(log, '\n') { row =>
        let line = trim(row);
        if starts_with(line, "ERROR ") {
            let end = find(line[6..], " ");
            assert(end >= 0);
            out.append("ERROR token=[redacted]");
            out.append(line[6 + end..]);
            out.push('\n');
            count++;
        }
    };
    print("errors=", count, "\n", out);
}
```


## Cloudflare story, prior art and what Wouter would likely value

**S/I:** Workers accepts Wasm modules and SIMD but does not provide native Goose pthread/worker behavior or a complete desktop OS runtime. Single-thread independent instances and explicit host capabilities are the viable local shape here. A Goose `thread_fn` does not automatically become a Durable Object: scheduling, queue transport, typed-value verification and durable delivery must be supplied by the host. This spike tested **local workerd only**, with static module imports, not an account deployment, Dynamic Workers, Durable Objects or R2. [Workers WebAssembly contract](https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers/runtime-apis/webassembly/).

**S/I:** Sofort already proved portable QuickJS snapshots, so this is an efficiency/backend choice, not the first pause/fork/move result. Golem's oplog-based durable Wasm and fork facilities are the closest software-runtime prior art; Cloudflare's Code Mode replay and Dynamic Workflows handle ordinary short generated scripts without preserving all machine state. Hyperlight competes on small hardware-isolated execution; E2B and others offer richer sandbox/process environments. Goose does not erase those competitors. [Golem durability](https://learn.golem.cloud/v1.6/concepts/durable-execution), [Golem forks](https://learn.golem.cloud/v1.6/develop/forking), [Cloudflare Dynamic Workflows](https://developers.cloudflare.com/dynamic-workers/usage/dynamic-workflows/), [Hyperlight](https://github.com/hyperlight-dev/hyperlight), [E2B pause/resume](https://e2b.dev/docs/sandbox/persistence). The wider, sourced competitor/roadmap analysis is in [04-cloudflare-signal.md](../primitive/04-cloudflare-signal.md).

**I:** The strongest short demo is: model generates a data-processing program; compile to a tiny cached capsule; compute a large partial result; park at a capability import; kill the executor; restore elsewhere; fork with two read-only replies; display module, live-page and snapshot bytes, with an effects ledger preventing duplicate committed writes. That would demonstrate a concrete systems improvement. “We mapped queues onto DOs” without durable effect/ownership semantics is mostly an integration sketch.

**I — Likely Wouter interests, inferred from the published priority order and design notes, not a claim about his private plans:**

* Preserve speed, safe stable references, in-place construction and the simple data-stack model. Bring measured code size, compile time, growth-check overhead, peak stack usage and a reproduction—not an abstract acquisition pitch.
* A real **32-bit representation contract** and a safe bounded-stack configuration tested natively first would address actual portability gaps. Stress every growth/construction path, including cached tops and C builders; a coarse post-hoc capacity check is insufficient.
* Compiler-known checkpoint boundaries could spill fixed locals/control state into a flat continuation and avoid a generic transform. That is a research direction, not implemented Goose functionality. Benchmark it against ordinary Asyncify and against a manually explicit state machine.
* Make the platform-specific runtime replaceable through a narrow documented host ABI; retain precise diagnostics and testable safety. The current field-union patch is evidence of a blocker, not a sensible upstream ABI proposal.
* Contribute a reproducible Wasm test target and small measured fixes first. Do not ask him to redesign the language around agents, surrender the ownership model, or equate using his language with a hiring/acquisition rationale.

**E:** A bounded prototype with safe fixed-stack growth checks and a documented target layout is a days-to-weeks investigation; a production port, generic continuations, durable effects and worker semantics are not established by this afternoon's adapter. For the user's weeks-scale career goal, keep Goose as a focused upstream collaboration/demo candidate while Sofort v1 retains its current JS/native-tools scope. If the experiment cannot beat a compiled C/Rust capsule or a QuickJS initialized-base delta on a realistic workload, the language-specific product story weakens sharply. Cloudflare shipping equivalent continuation support would further reduce the integration wedge.

## Reproduction commands and evidence

**M:** All commands run from `/Users/noah/code/sofort-spikes/goose` unless noted. Third-party clone remained clean. Python scripts are always run with `-I`. The scripts contain full argument vectors; the appendix embeds the final build vectors, raw samples, hashes and results. No existing research/implementation files need modification.

```sh
mkdir -p /Users/noah/code/sofort-spikes/goose
# upstream/ did not exist before this clone:
git clone https://github.com/aardappel/goose /Users/noah/code/sofort-spikes/goose/upstream
# Pin the exact measured source in a fresh reproduction:
git -C upstream checkout e76d5c9f7f0a17d83c12a405fbb2513094d03985
# Read CMakeLists.txt and referenced cmake/libtcc.cmake / cmake/package.cmake first.
cmake -S upstream -B build -G Ninja -DCMAKE_BUILD_TYPE=Release -DGOOSE_BUILTIN_LIBS=
cmake --build build --parallel 3
build/goose version
build/goose --emit-runtime out/goose_runtime.c
for t in json_transform aggregate text compute snapshot; do
  build/goose --stdlib upstream/stdlib -o out/$t.c tasks/$t.goose
done
clang -O2 out/goose_runtime.c -c -o out/goose_runtime.o
for t in json_transform aggregate text compute snapshot; do
  clang -O2 out/$t.c out/goose_runtime.o native_hooks.c -lm -pthread -o out/$t-native
  zig cc -target aarch64-linux-musl -O2 -static -s out/$t.c out/goose_runtime.c native_hooks.c -lm -pthread -o out/$t-musl
done

/tmp/portability-wasm/wasi-sdk-34.0-arm64-macos/bin/clang -O2 -c out/goose_runtime.c -o out/stock-runtime.o

node smoke.mjs

curl -fsS http://127.0.0.1:18753/ -o logs/workerd-result.json
```
